r_{t}\leq\sum_{k\in[K]}|\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}|
. For each arm
k\in[K]
,
due to Assumption
4 (b)
and
5 (a)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}|\right]\leq\sqrt{(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}}\leq m_{\theta}\sqrt{L_{3}},

(13)

which, together with the choice of
\gamma_{1}
in (
10
), implies
\sum_{t=1}^{\gamma_{1}}\mathbb{E}[r_{t}]\leq CC_{\gamma_{1}}(\sigma^{2}\vee 1)s_{0}\log(dT)
.

Stage 2.
Let
t\in(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}]
. For some
m\in\{1,\ldots,\gamma_{2}/\gamma_{1}\}
, we have
t\in(m\gamma_{1},(m+1)\gamma_{1}]
. Let

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}_{i,j}:=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(j)},\quad\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i,j}:=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(i)}_{m\gamma_{1}}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(j)}_{m\gamma_{1}},\quad\text{ for }i,j\in[K].

Due to the definition of
r_{t}
in (
7
) and
A_{t}
in Definition
3
, we have

\displaystyle r_{t}

\displaystyle=|\boldsymbol{u}_{A_{t}^{*},A_{t}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}|\mathbbm{1}\{\textup{sgn}(\boldsymbol{u}_{A_{t}^{*},A_{t}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t})\neq\textup{sgn}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{A_{t}^{*},A_{t}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t})\},

where for
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
,
\textup{sgn}(\tau):=\mathbbm{1}\{\tau>0\}-\mathbbm{1}\{\tau<0\}
. This is because if
r_{t}=0
, both sides equal zero; while if
r_{t}>0
, then
\boldsymbol{u}_{A_{t}^{*},A_{t}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}>0
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{A_{t}^{*},A_{t}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\leq 0
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle r_{t}\leq\sum_{i\neq j\in[K]}|\boldsymbol{u}_{i,j}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}|\mathbbm{1}\{\textup{sgn}(\boldsymbol{u}_{i,j}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t})\neq\textup{sgn}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i,j}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t})\}.

Since
\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i,j}\in\mathcal{F}_{m\gamma_{1}}
and
\boldsymbol{X}_{t}
is independent from
\mathcal{F}_{m\gamma_{1}}
, due to Assumption
4 (a)
and by applying Lemma
33
conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{m\gamma_{1}}
, we have that for
i\neq j\in[K]
, almost surely (a.s.),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\boldsymbol{u}_{i,j}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}|\mathbbm{1}\{\textup{sgn}(\boldsymbol{u}_{i,j}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t})\neq\textup{sgn}(\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i,j}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t})\}|\mathcal{F}_{m\gamma_{1}}\right]\leq C{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{i,j}-\hat{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i,j}\|_{2}^{2}}/{\|\boldsymbol{u}_{i,j}\|_{2}}.

Due to Assumption
5 (b)
,
\|\boldsymbol{u}_{i,j}\|_{2}\geq L_{4}^{-1}
for
i\neq j\in[K]
. By the triangle inequality, a.s.,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[r_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{m\gamma_{1}}\right]\leq C\sum_{k\in[K]}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(k)}_{m\gamma_{1}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}\|_{2}^{2}.

Recall the event
\mathcal{E}_{m\gamma_{1}}\in\mathcal{F}_{m\gamma_{1}}
in Theorem
11
. By definition, a.s.,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[r_{t}\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{E}_{m\gamma_{1}}\}|\mathcal{F}_{m\gamma_{1}}]\leq C\sum_{k\in[K]}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(k)}_{m\gamma_{1}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{E}_{m\gamma_{1}}\}\leq C\sigma^{2}\frac{s_{0}\log(dT)}{m\gamma_{1}}.

Further, on the event
\mathcal{E}_{m\gamma_{1}}^{c}
, we use the bound:
r_{t}\leq\sum_{k\in[K]}|\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}|
. Since
X_{t}
is independent from
\mathcal{F}_{m\gamma_{1}}
, a.s.,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[r_{t}\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{E}_{m\gamma_{1}}^{c}\}|\mathcal{F}_{m\gamma_{1}}]=\sum_{k\in[K]}\mathbb{E}\left[|\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}|\right]\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{E}_{m\gamma_{1}}^{c}\}\leq C\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{E}_{m\gamma_{1}}^{c}\},

where we use (
13
) in the last step.
Due to Theorem
11
(i) and by the law of total expectation,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[r_{t}\right]\leq C\sigma^{2}\frac{s_{0}\log(dT)}{m\gamma_{1}}+C\frac{1}{T},

which implies the following upper bound on the cumulative regret in Stage 2:

\displaystyle\sum_{t=\gamma_{1}+1}^{\gamma_{2}}\mathbb{E}[r_{t}]\leq C\sum_{m=1}^{\gamma_{2}/\gamma_{1}}\gamma_{1}\left(\sigma^{2}\frac{s_{0}\log(dT)}{m\gamma_{1}}+\frac{1}{T}\right)\leq C(\sigma^{2}\vee 1)\log(\gamma_{2}/\gamma_{1})s_{0}\log(dT).

Due to the choice of
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
in (
10
),
\sum_{t=\gamma_{1}+1}^{\gamma_{2}}\mathbb{E}[r_{t}]\leq C(\sigma^{2}\vee 1)\log(s_{0})s_{0}\log(dT)
.

Stage 3.

Let
t\in(\gamma_{2},T]
. Then for some
m\in\{1,\ldots,\lfloor T/\gamma_{2}\rfloor\}
, we have
t\in(m\gamma_{2},(m+1)\gamma_{2}]
. Recall the event
\mathcal{A}_{m\gamma_{2}}
in Theorem
11
(ii).
By similar arguments as for Stage 2,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[r_{t}]\leq C\sum_{k\in[K]}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{m\gamma_{2}}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbbm{1}{\{\mathcal{A}_{m\gamma_{2}}\}}\right]\right)+C\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{m\gamma_{2}}^{c}).

Then due to Theorem
11
(ii),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[r_{t}]\leq C\sum_{k\in[K]}\left(\sum_{j\in S_{k}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}_{j})^{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}_{j}|\leq 2\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{m\gamma_{2}}\right\}+\frac{\sigma^{2}s_{0}}{m\gamma_{2}}\right)+\frac{C}{T}.

It is elementary to see that

\sum_{m=1}^{\lfloor T/\gamma_{2}\rfloor}\gamma_{2}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}s_{0}}{m\gamma_{2}}+\frac{1}{T}\right)\leq C(\sigma^{2}\vee 1)s_{0}\log T
.
Further, due to Lemma
2
and the choice of
\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{t}
in (
9
), we have

\sum_{k\in[K]}\sum_{m=1}^{\lfloor T/\gamma_{2}\rfloor}\gamma_{2}\sum_{j\in S_{k}}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}_{j})^{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{|\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}_{j}|\leq 2\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{m\gamma_{2}}\right\}\leq C\sigma^{2}s_{0}\log(dT).

Thus,
\sum_{t=\gamma_{2}+1}^{T}\mathbb{E}[r_{t}]\leq C(\sigma^{2}\vee 1)s_{0}\log(dT)
. The proof is then complete by combining the upper bounds on the cumulative regret from each stage.
∎

4
Conclusion

We study the problem of high-dimensional stochastic linear contextual bandits under sparsity constraints. To isolate the effect of estimation methods, we first analyze a related sequential estimation problem in which no arm selection is involved. We show that while the Lasso estimator is minimax optimal in the
fixed-time
setting, it is suboptimal in terms of cumulative estimation error in the sequential setting. In contrast, OPT-Lasso estimators attain the minimax rate in the sequential regime. Building on these insights, we propose a three-stage algorithm for the contextual bandit problem that primarily relies on OPT-Lasso estimators. We derive upper bounds on its cumulative regret and matching lower bounds over all permissible policies, thereby showing that the algorithm is nearly minimax optimal—up to a
\log(s_{0})
factor—over the full horizon, and exactly minimax optimal if an initial short transient phase is excluded.

Several directions remain for future investigation. First, it is an open question whether the
\log s_{0}
gap in the overall regret bound can be eliminated. Second, developing principled methods for tuning parameter selection in the proposed three-stage algorithm is of practical importance. Third, extending the framework to nonlinear models with high-dimensional covariates is an interesting direction for future research.

A
Lasso and OPT-Lasso: Deterministic Analysis

In this Appendix, we consider the following
deterministic
linear regression model

\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{Z}\boldsymbol{\theta}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon},

where
\boldsymbol{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
is a deterministic design matrix,
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a deterministic noise vector, and
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{d})^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an unknown vector. We review several well-known results for Lasso estimators and derive new results for OPT-Lasso estimators. Specifically, denote by

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{n}:=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{Y},\lambda_{n}),\quad\text{ and }\quad\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}:=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{Z},\boldsymbol{Y},\lambda_{n},\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{n})

the Lasso estimator (see Definition
1
) and the OPT-Lasso estimator (see Definition
2
) respectively,
where
\lambda_{n},\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}>0
are to be specified.

Denote by
S:=\{j\in[d]:\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}\neq 0\}
the support of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
and let
s_{0}=|S|\vee 1
. Further, denote by
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{n}=n^{-1}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
the sample covariance matrix. Note that
\boldsymbol{Z}_{S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}_{S}/n=(\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{n})_{S,S}
.

Definition 4
.

A matrix
\boldsymbol{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
is said to satisfy the restricted eigenvalue condition
\textup{RE}(s,\kappa,\rho)
for some
s\in[d]
and
\kappa,\rho>0
if

n^{-1}\|\boldsymbol{Z}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\rho\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2},\quad\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{C}(s,\kappa),

where we define

\mathcal{C}(s,\kappa):=\{\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{\boldsymbol{0}_{d}\}:\text{there exists }J\subseteq[d]\text{ such that }|J|=s,\text{ and }\|\boldsymbol{v}_{J^{c}}\|_{1}\leq\kappa\|\boldsymbol{v}_{J}\|_{1}\}.

Remark 7
.

The above definition is adapted from
[
44
, Definition 7.12]
.

The following
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
, and
\ell_{\infty}
bounds on the estimation error of the Lasso estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{n}
are well-known and are provided here for convenient reference.

Lemma 12
.

Let
a>0
and
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be any invertible matrix. Assume that
\boldsymbol{Z}
satisfies the
\textup{RE}(s_{0},3,a)
condition, and that

\lambda_{n}\geq 2\|\boldsymbol{Z}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}/n\|_{\infty}
. Then

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{1}\leq{8s_{0}\lambda_{n}}/{a},

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\leq{3\sqrt{s_{0}}\lambda_{n}}/{a},

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{\infty}\leq|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{1}\left(4+8|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{A}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{\textup{max}}s_{0}/a\right)\lambda_{n}.

The
\ell_{1}
bound follows from Theorem 6.1 in
[
12
]
, while the
\ell_{2}
bound from Theorem 7.13 (a) in
[
45
]
. The
\ell_{\infty}
bound
is derived from Lemma 4.1 in
[
42
]
, combined with the
\ell_{1}
bound above.
∎

Recall the definition of
\hat{S}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}:=\{j\in[d]:|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{n})_{j}|>\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}\}
in Definition
2
. Define the following set

S_{n}^{\textup{str}}:=\{j\in[d]:|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}|>2\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}\},

which contains the indices of components of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
whose magnitudes exceed
2\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}
.

Lemma 13
.

If
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}
, then
S_{n}^{\textup{str}}\subseteq\hat{S}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}\subseteq S
.

Let
j\in S_{n}^{\textup{str}}
and
k\in\hat{S}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}
. Since
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}
, by triangle inequality and definition, we have

|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{n})_{j}|\geq|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}|-\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}>\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{n},\qquad|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{k}|\geq|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{n})_{k}|-\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}>0,

which implies
j\in\hat{S}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}
and
k\in S
. The proof is complete.
∎

Next, we provide an instance-specific upper bound on the
\ell_{2}
estimation error of the OPT-Lasso estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}
.

Theorem 14
.

Assume
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}
.

i)

If the support
S
of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is an empty set, then

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}=0
;

ii)

If the support
S
of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is not an empty set, assume further that for some
0<a<b
,

a\leq\lambda_{\min}\left((\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{n})_{S,S}\right)\leq\lambda_{\max}\left((\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{n})_{S,S}\right)\leq b.

Then we have

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}\leq(2b/a+1)\sum_{j\in S}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}|\leq 2\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}\right\}+(2/a^{2})\|\boldsymbol{Z}_{S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}/n\|_{2}^{2}.

By Lemma
13
, we have
S_{n}^{\textup{str}}\subseteq\hat{S}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}\subseteq S
. By definition, we have

\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\left\|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta})_{S}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\left\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{S\backslash\hat{S}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta})_{\hat{S}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}}\right\|_{2}^{2}

(14)

If
S
is an empty set, then
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}=\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{0}_{d}
.

If
S
is not empty and
\hat{S}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}
is an empty set, then
S_{n}^{\textup{str}}
must be empty, which implies that
|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}|\leq 2\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}
for all
j\in[d]
. Thus by (
14
), we have

\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{S}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{j\in S}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\left|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}\right|\leq 2\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}\right\}.

Now, we focus on the case that neither
S
nor
\hat{S}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}
is empty, and we bound the two terms on the right-hand side of (
14
). For the first term in (
14
), by definition, we have

\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{S\backslash\hat{S}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{S\backslash S_{n}^{\textup{str}}}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{j\in S}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\left|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}\right|\leq 2\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}\right\}.

(15)

Next, we bound the second term in (
14
). To simplify notations, denote
\hat{S}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}
by
\hat{S}_{n}
. Since

\hat{S}_{n}\subseteq S
, we have

\lambda_{\min}\left((\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{n})_{\hat{S}_{n},\hat{S}_{n}}\right)\geq\lambda_{\min}\left((\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{n})_{S,S}\right)\geq a>0,

which in particular implies that
\boldsymbol{Z}_{\hat{S}_{n}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}_{\hat{S}_{n}}
is invertible. By the definition of OPT-Lasso (see Definition
2
), we have

\displaystyle(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n})_{\hat{S}_{n}}

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{Z}_{\hat{S}_{n}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}_{\hat{S}_{n}}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{Z}_{\hat{S}_{n}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Y}=\left(\boldsymbol{Z}_{\hat{S}_{n}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}_{\hat{S}_{n}}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{Z}_{\hat{S}_{n}}^{\prime}\left(\boldsymbol{Z}_{\hat{S}_{n}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\hat{S}_{n}}+\boldsymbol{Z}_{S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\right)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\hat{S}_{n}}+\left(\boldsymbol{Z}_{\hat{S}_{n}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}_{\hat{S}_{n}}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{Z}_{\hat{S}_{n}}^{\prime}\left(\boldsymbol{Z}_{S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\right),

which implies that

\left\|\boldsymbol{Z}_{\hat{S}_{n}}\left((\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta})_{\hat{S}_{n}}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}=\left\|\mathcal{P}_{\hat{S}_{n}}\left(\boldsymbol{Z}_{S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2},

(16)

where for a non-empty set
A\subseteq[S]
, we define

\mathcal{P}_{A}:=\boldsymbol{Z}_{A}\left(\boldsymbol{Z}_{A}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}_{A}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{Z}_{A}^{\prime}
.

For the left-hand side of (
16
), we have

\begin{split}\left\|\boldsymbol{Z}_{\hat{S}_{n}}\left((\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta})_{\hat{S}_{n}}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}&\geq n\lambda_{\min}\left((\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{n})_{\hat{S}_{n},\hat{S}_{n}}\right)\cdot\left\|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta})_{\hat{S}_{n}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\\
&\geq na\left\|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta})_{\hat{S}_{n}}\right\|_{2}^{2}.\end{split}

(17)

For the right-hand side of (
16
), we first note that
\mathcal{P}_{A}
is the projection matrix onto the column space of
Z_{A}
.
Since
\hat{S}_{n}\subseteq S
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{P}_{\hat{S}_{n}}\left(\boldsymbol{Z}_{S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\left\|\mathcal{P}_{\hat{S}_{n}}\boldsymbol{Z}_{S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+2\left\|\mathcal{P}_{\hat{S}_{n}}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\right\|_{2}^{2}

(18)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|\boldsymbol{Z}_{S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+2\left\|\mathcal{P}_{S}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2n\lambda_{\max}\left((\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{n})_{S\backslash\hat{S}_{n},S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}}\right)\left\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+2n[\lambda_{\min}\left((\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{n})_{S,S}\right)]^{-1}\|\boldsymbol{Z}_{S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}/n\|_{2}^{2}.

where the first inequality is due to the triangle inequality, the second inequality follows from the properties of projection matrices, and the final inequality follows from the definition of the eigenvalues.

Note that if
S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}
is empty, the first term above is understood to be zero by convention. If
S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}
is empty,
we have

\lambda_{\max}\left((\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{n})_{S\backslash\hat{S}_{n},S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}}\right)\leq\lambda_{\max}\left((\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{n})_{S,S}\right)\leq b
.
Therefore by (
16
), (
17
), and (
18
), we have

\displaystyle\left\|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta})_{\hat{S}_{n}}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2b}{a}\left\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{S\backslash\hat{S}_{n}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{2}{a^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{Z}_{S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}/n\|_{2}^{2}.

Then the proof is complete due to (
15
) and (
14
).
∎

B
Lower Bounds on Bayes Risks

In this appendix, we consider the following linear model
\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{Z}\boldsymbol{\theta}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
within the Bayesian framework, where
\boldsymbol{Z}=(\boldsymbol{Z}_{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{Z}_{n})^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
is a random design matrix with i.i.d. rows,
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}=(\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{n})^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a noise vector with i.i.d. entries distributed as
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
, and
\boldsymbol{\theta}=(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\theta}_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a random vector with some prior distribution to be specified.

Our goal is to derive lower bounds on the Bayes risks for estimating certain functionals of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
based on
\boldsymbol{Y}
and
\boldsymbol{Z}
, under
two
types of prior distributions on
\boldsymbol{\theta}
. Note that each estimator belongs to
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{n,d}
, the set of all measurable functions from
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Assumption 6
.

For some constant
L>1
, we have
L^{-1}\leq\lambda_{\min}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\right)\leq\lambda_{\max}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\right)\leq L
.

First, we define a family of prior distributions for
\boldsymbol{\theta}
, under which the support of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is known. For
s\geq 2
and
r>0
, define a probability density function (pdf) with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{s}
as follows: for
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{s}
,

\rho_{s,r}(\boldsymbol{u}):=\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{r}(\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2})}{A_{s}\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}^{s-1}},\quad\text{ where }\;\tilde{\rho}_{r}(\tau):=4r^{-1}\sin^{2}(2\pi r^{-1}\tau)\mathbbm{1}\{r/2\leq\tau\leq r\},

where
A_{s}
is the Lebesgue area of the
s
dimensional unit sphere
\mathcal{S}^{s-1}
.

Definition 5
.

Let
2\leq s\leq d
and
r>0
. Define
\omega_{1}(s,r)
as the distribution of the random vector
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
given by the following construction:
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}=0
for
j\in\{s+1,\ldots,d\}
, and
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{[s]}
has the Lebesgue density
\rho_{s,r}
.

Lemma 15
.

Let
2\leq s\leq d
and
r>0
.
Suppose that Assumption
6
holds, and that
\boldsymbol{\theta}
has the prior distribution
\omega_{1}(s,r)
. Then, for some constant
C>0
, depending only on
r
, we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{n,d}}\;\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\omega_{1}(s,r)}\left(\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\geq\frac{\sigma^{2}s}{C(n+\sigma^{2}s)},

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{n,d}}\;\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\omega_{1}(s,r)}\left(\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}-\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\geq\frac{\sigma^{2}s}{C(n+\sigma^{2}s)}.

Under the prior distribution
\omega_{1}(s,r)
,
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is supported on
[s]
with probability
1
, which implies the same property holds for the posterior means:
\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\theta}|Y,Z]
and

\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\theta}/\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\;|Y,Z]
. Then, the results follow from Theorem 31 in
[
36
]
.
∎

Next, we define the second family of prior distributions for
\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which is supported on finitely many
\mathbb{R}^{d}
vectors. We begin with a lemma.

Lemma 16
.

Let
3\leq s\leq d
and
r,\delta>0
. There exists
M\in\mathbb{N}
and vectors
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that for any indexes
i\neq j\in[M]
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}\right\|_{0}=s,\quad\|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}=r^{2}+2\delta^{2},\quad\delta^{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 8\delta^{2},\quad\log M\geq\mathcal{L}_{d,s}.

(19)

where
\mathcal{L}_{d,s}:=\frac{s-1}{2}\log\frac{d-s}{(s-1)/2}
.

By
Lemma 5 in
[
29
]
, there exists

M\in\mathbb{N}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{M}\in\{-1,0,1\}^{d-1}
such that for any indexes
i\neq j\in[M]
it satisfies

\left\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}\right\|_{0}=s-1,\quad\rho(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i},\tilde{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j})\geq\frac{s-1}{2},\quad\log M\geq\frac{s-1}{2}\log\frac{d-s}{(s-1)/2}=\mathcal{L}_{d,s},

where
\rho(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\mathbbm{1}\{u_{k}\neq v_{k}\}
for
\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the Hamming distance. In particular, for
i\neq j\in[M]
, we have

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}\|_{2}=\sqrt{s-1},\quad\text{ and }\quad\sqrt{\frac{s-1}{2}}\leq\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}\|_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{s-1}.

Finally, for each
i\in[M]
, define the following vector:

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}=(r,\sqrt{\frac{2}{s-1}}\delta\tilde{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}^{\prime})^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

It is elementary to see that
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{M}
satisfy the desired property. The proof is complete.
∎

Definition 6
.

Let
3\leq s\leq d
and
r,\delta>0
. Let
\mathcal{A}(s,r,\delta):=\{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{M}\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be any set of vectors such that (
19
) holds. Define
\omega_{2}(s,r,\delta)
as the discrete uniform distribution over the set
\mathcal{A}(s,r,\delta)
.

Lemma 17
.

Let
3\leq s\leq(d+2)/3
,
r>0
, and
N\geq n
. Suppose that Assumption
6
holds, and that
\boldsymbol{\theta}
has the prior distribution
\omega_{2}(s,r,\delta)
, where

\displaystyle\delta^{2}:=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{16LN}\mathcal{L}_{d,s},

(20)

where
L
appears in Assumption
6
and
\mathcal{L}_{d,s}
in Lemma
16
. Then there exist constants
C>0
, depending only on
L
, such that

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{n,d}}\;\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\omega_{2}}\left(\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\geq\frac{\sigma^{2}}{CN}s\log\frac{d}{s},

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{n,d}}\;\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\omega_{2}}\left(\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}-\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)\geq\frac{1}{r^{2}+2\delta^{2}}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{CN}s\log\frac{d}{s}.

Denote by
\mathcal{F}_{M}
the collection of all measurable function from
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\ \mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
to
[M]
, where
M
is the size of the support of the prior distribution
\omega_{2}(s,r,\delta)
.

Step 1: reduction to a hypothesis testing problem.
Let
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}
be any estimator of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
. Define the following procedure
\hat{\psi}\in\mathcal{F}_{M}
:

\hat{\psi}(\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{Z}):=\argmin_{j\in[M]}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}\|_{2},

with ties resolved in a fixed, deterministic manner. For
j\in[M]
, on the event
\{\hat{\psi}(\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{Z})\neq j\}
, by definition, there exits some
j^{\prime}\neq j\in[M]
such that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}\|_{2}\geq\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j^{\prime}}\|_{2},

which, by the triangle inequality and (
19
), implies that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}\|_{2}\geq\|\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j^{\prime}}\|_{2}-\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}\|_{2}\geq\delta-\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}\|_{2}.

Thus, on the event
\{\hat{\psi}(\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{Z})\neq j\}
, we have
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\delta^{2}/4
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\omega_{2}}(\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\delta^{2}}{4}\inf_{\psi\in\mathcal{F}_{M}}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}}(\psi(\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{Z})\neq i),

(21)

where
\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}}
denotes the conditional distribution of
(\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{Z})
on the event
\{\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}\}
.

By the same argument, we also have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\omega_{2}}\left(\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{n}-\frac{\boldsymbol{\theta}}{\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\delta^{2}}{4(r^{2}+2\delta^{2})}\inf_{\psi\in\mathcal{F}_{M}}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}}(\psi(\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{Z})\neq i).

(22)

Step 2: lower bound the average testing error.

By Fano’s inequality (see
[
31
, Theorem 4.10]
), we have

\inf_{\psi\in\mathcal{F}_{M}}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}}(\psi(\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{Z})\neq i)\geq 1-\frac{\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in[M]}D(\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}}||\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}})+\log 2}{\log M},

where
D(P||Q)=\int\log(dP/dQ)dP
is the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence between two distributions
P
and
Q
. For
i\neq j\in[M]
, since
\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n}
are i.i.d. distributed as
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
,

\displaystyle\log\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}}}{d\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}}}(\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{Z})=\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\left[2\boldsymbol{Y}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j})-\|\boldsymbol{Z}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\boldsymbol{Z}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\right],

which implies that
D(\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}}||\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}})=(2\sigma^{2})^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}}\left[\|\boldsymbol{Z}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j})\|_{2}^{2}\right]
. Then, due to Assumption
6
and (
19
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in[M]}D(\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}}||\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}})\leq\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in[M]}\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}8\delta^{2}nL=\frac{4\delta^{2}nL}{\sigma^{2}}.

Then due to the definition of
\delta^{2}
in (
20
) and since
N>n
and
\log M\geq\mathcal{L}_{d,s}
(see (
19
)),

\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{i,j\in[M]}D(\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}}||\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{j}})\leq\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{L}_{d,s}\leq\frac{1}{4}\log M.

Since
3\leq s\leq(d+2)/3
, we have
\log(M)\geq\mathcal{L}_{d,s}\geq 2\log(2)
, and thus

\inf_{\psi\in\mathcal{F}_{M}}\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}}(\psi(\boldsymbol{Y},\boldsymbol{Z})\neq i)\geq 1-\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1}{4}.

Since
3\leq s\leq(d+2)/3
, we further have

\delta^{2}\geq\frac{\sigma^{2}}{16LN}\frac{s}{4}\log\left(\frac{2d}{3s}\right),\quad\text{ and }\quad\frac{2d}{3s}\geq\left(\frac{d}{s}\right)^{1/3}.

Then the proof is complete in view of (
21
) and (
22
).
∎

C
Proofs for the Sequential Estimation Problem

C.1
Proof of Theorem
1

We begin with a Lemma that provides a high probability upper bound on the
\ell_{\infty}
estimation error of Lasso estimators.

Lemma 18
.

Suppose that Assumption
1
holds, and that
d\geq(2L_{0}+1)s_{0}+2
. There exist constants
C_{0},\kappa_{1}>0
depending only on
L_{0}
and a constant
C>0
depending only on
C_{0},L_{0}
, such that if we set the regularization parameter
\lambda_{t}
as follows:

\lambda_{t}=C_{0}\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\log(dt)}{t}},

then for
t\geq\kappa_{1}s_{0}\log d
, with probability at least
1-{C}de^{-t/{C}}-{C}/t
, we have

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{\infty}\leq C\lambda_{t}.

This result follows essentially from Theorem 5.1 in
[
9
]
, with a minor modification that we outline below. Specifically, let

\displaystyle k=1,\quad m=L_{0}s_{0},\quad\rho_{*}=L_{0}^{-1},\quad\eta_{2}=0.05,\quad\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon_{2}=0.05,\quad\epsilon_{3}=\epsilon_{4}=\epsilon_{2}^{2}/16.

Then, if Assumption
1
holds and
d\geq(2L_{0}+1)s_{0}+2
, there exists a large enough constant
\kappa_{1}>0
depending only on
L_{0}
such that for all
t\geq\kappa_{1}s_{0}\log(d)
,

i)

\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is invertible, and for
j\in[d]
,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j,j}\leq 1
;

ii)

with
\tau_{*}=(1-\epsilon_{1}-\epsilon_{2})^{2},\tau^{*}=(1+\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2})^{2}
, we have

s_{0}+k<\frac{(1-\eta_{2})^{2}2m}{(1+\eta_{2})^{2}\left((\tau^{*}/\tau_{*})\phi_{\text{cond}}(m+k,S,\boldsymbol{\Sigma})-1\right)},

where
\phi_{\text{cond}}(m+k,S,\boldsymbol{\Sigma}):=\max_{A\subseteq[d]:|A\backslash S|\leq 1\vee(L_{0}s_{0}+1)}\frac{\lambda_{\max}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{A,A}\right)}{\lambda_{\min}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{A,A}\right)}
;

iii)

\rho_{*}\leq\min_{A\subseteq[d]:|A\backslash S|=L_{0}s_{0}+1}\lambda_{\min}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{A,A}\right)
and
\lambda_{0}\sqrt{s_{*}}\leq 1
, where

\lambda_{0}=\sqrt{2(1+4L_{0}^{2})\frac{\log(8dt)}{t}},\quad\text{ and }\quad s_{*}=s_{0}+m+k;

iv)

finally,

\displaystyle 2(m+k)+s_{0}+1\leq(t-1)\wedge(d+1),\quad\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}<1,\quad\epsilon_{3}+\epsilon_{4}=\epsilon_{2}^{2}/8,

\displaystyle s_{0}+m+k+1\leq(d+1)\wedge(\epsilon_{1}^{2}t/2),\quad\log\binom{d-s_{0}}{m+k}\leq\epsilon_{3}t.

That is, the conditions (3.5)-(3.8) in Assumption 3.1 of
[
9
]
hold with a modified
\lambda_{0}
defined above.

Then, following the comment after Proposition A.6 of
[
9
]
, if we replace
L_{k}=\sqrt{2\log(d/k)}
by
L_{k}=\sqrt{2(1+4L_{0}^{2})\log(dt)}
in Proposition A.6 of
[
9
]
, we have
\mathbb{P}\left(\Omega_{noise}^{(1)}\cap\Omega_{noise}^{(2)}\right)\geq 1-t^{-1}
. The remainder of the argument closely follows the proof of Theorem 5.1 in
[
9
]
and is therefore omitted.
∎

Lemma 19
.

Suppose that Assumption
1 (c)
holds, and that the support
S
of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is non-empty. Let
C=(16L_{0}^{2})^{2}
.
Then, for each
t\in[T]
, if
t\geq Cs_{0}
, then with probability at least
1-2e^{-t/(2C^{2})}
,

\frac{L_{0}^{-1}}{2}\leq\lambda_{\min}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}}{t}\right)\leq\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}}{t}\right)\leq\frac{3L_{0}}{2},

where
\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}
is the submatrix of
\boldsymbol{X}_{[t]}
with columns indexed by
S
.

Let
\boldsymbol{Z}:=\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}
.
Note that
\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{Z}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}/t]=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S,S}
. By Example 6.3 in
[
45
]
, with probability at least
1-2e^{-t\delta^{2}/2}
,

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\frac{\boldsymbol{Z}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}}{t}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S,S}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\textup{OP}}\leq\lambda_{\max}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S,S}\right)\left(2\sqrt{\frac{s_{0}}{t}}+2\delta+\left(\sqrt{\frac{s_{0}}{t}}+\delta\right)^{2}\right),

where
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\textup{OP}}
denotes the operator norm of a matrix.

Let
C=(16L_{0}^{2})^{2}
and
\delta=1/C
. Then, if
t\geq Cs_{0}
, due to Assumption
1 (c)
, with probability at least
1-2e^{-t/(2C^{2})}
,

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|{\boldsymbol{Z}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}}/{t}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S,S}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\textup{OP}}\leq 1/(2L_{0})
. Then the proof is complete due to Assumption
1 (c)
and the variational characterization of eigenvalues.
∎

Finally, we prove Theorem
1
.

For
t\in[T]
, denote by
\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}
the submatrix of
\boldsymbol{X}_{[t]}
with columns indexed by
S
.
By Lemma
18
and Lemma
19
, there exist constants
\kappa_{1},C_{0},C_{0}^{\textup{hard}}>0
depending only on
L_{0}
, and a large enough constant
C>0
depending only on
L_{0},C_{0}
, such that for all
t\geq\kappa_{1}s_{0}\log d
, the events

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1,t}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\frac{L_{0}^{-1}}{2}\leq\lambda_{\min}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}}{t}\right)\leq\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}}{t}\right)\leq\frac{3L_{0}}{2}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2,t}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{t}\right\},

satisfy the following:

\mathbb{P}\left((\mathcal{A}_{1,t}\cap\mathcal{A}_{2,t})^{c}\right)\leq Cde^{-t/C}+C/t
.

First, we fix some
t\geq\kappa_{1}s_{0}\log d
and bound the
\ell_{2}
estimation error of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{t}
on the event
\mathcal{A}_{1,t}\cap\mathcal{A}_{2,t}
. By Theorem
14
, on the event
\mathcal{A}_{1,t}\cap\mathcal{A}_{2,t}
, there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
L_{0}
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\|_{2}^{2}\mathbbm{1}{\{\mathcal{A}_{1,t}\cap\mathcal{A}_{2,t}\}}\right]\leq C\sum_{j\in S}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}|\leq 2\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{t}\right\}+C\mathbb{E}\left[\|\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{[t]}\|_{2}^{2}\right],

where
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{[t]}=(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{t})^{\prime}
.
Due to Assumption
1
, we have
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j,j}\leq 1
for
j\in[d]
, and thus

\mathbb{E}\left[\|\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{[t]}\|_{2}^{2}\right]=\text{trace}(\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{[t]}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{[t]}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}])=\sigma^{2}t\times\text{trace}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S,S})\leq\sigma^{2}s_{0}t.

Therefore, for
t\geq\kappa_{1}s_{0}\log d
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\|_{2}^{2}\wedge\xi\right]^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\right\|_{2}^{2}\mathbbm{1}{\{\mathcal{A}_{1,t}\cap\mathcal{A}_{2,t}\}}\right]+\xi\mathbb{P}\left((\mathcal{A}_{1,t}\cap\mathcal{A}_{2,t})^{c}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{j\in S}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}|\leq 2\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{t}\right\}+C\sigma^{2}s_{0}/t+\xi(Cde^{-t/C}+C/t),

which completes the proof for the first claim.

Next, we focus on the cumulative estimation error. Let
\widetilde{C}>0
be a constant to be specified, and define
t_{0}=\lceil(\widetilde{C}\vee\kappa_{1})s_{0}\log d\rceil
. For
1\leq t\leq t_{0}
, we bound each estimation error by
\xi
:

\sum_{t=1}^{t_{0}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}\wedge\xi\right]\leq\xi(\widetilde{C}\vee\kappa_{1})s_{0}\log d.

For
t>t_{0}
, we use the first claim: for some constant
C>0
depending on
\kappa_{1},L_{0},C_{0},C_{0}^{\textup{hard}}
, but not on
\widetilde{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=t_{0}+1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{OPT}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}\wedge\xi\right]

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{j\in S}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}|\leq\sqrt{\frac{C\sigma^{2}\log(dt)}{t}}\right\}+C\sigma^{2}\sum_{t=2}^{T}s_{0}/t

\displaystyle+\xi\sum_{t=t_{0}+1}^{T}Cde^{-t/C}+\xi\sum_{t=2}^{T}C/t.

For the first term on the right-hand side, by Lemma
2
with
b=C\sigma^{2}\log(dT)
,

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{j\in S}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}^{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{j}|\leq\sqrt{\frac{C\sigma^{2}\log(dt)}{t}}\right\}\leq C\sigma^{2}s_{0}(\log d+\log T).

For the second and fourth terms, note that
\sum_{t=2}^{T}1/t\leq\int_{1}^{T}(1/x)dx\leq\log T
. Finally, for the third term, if we choose
\widetilde{C}=C
, we have

\sum_{t=t_{0}+1}^{T}de^{-t/C}\leq\int_{C\log d}^{+\infty}e^{-(t-C\log d)/C}dt=C.

Then the proof is complete.

∎

C.2
Proof of Theorem
3

Recall the two families of distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
:
\omega_{1}(\cdot)
in Definition
5
and
\omega_{2}(\cdot)
in Definition
6
.

Let
\{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t},t\in[T]\}
be any sequence of permissible estimators.

Step 1. Lower bound the supremum risk by the average risk.
Let
\omega
be a distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined as an equally weighted mixture of the following two distributions:

\displaystyle\omega_{1}(s_{0},\sqrt{\xi/16})\quad\text{ and }\quad\omega_{2}(s_{0},\sqrt{\xi/32},\tilde{\delta})\;\;\text{ with }\tilde{\delta}^{2}:=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{16L_{0}T}\frac{s_{0}-1}{2}\log\left(\frac{d-s_{0}}{(s_{0}-1)/2}\right).

Importantly,
\omega
does not depend on
t
, and both
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
are supported on
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{d}[s_{0}]
, where we omit the arguments of
\omega_{1}(\cdot)
and
\omega_{2}(\cdot)
for simplicity. Thus,

\displaystyle 2\sup_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\in\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{d}[s_{0}]}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}\wedge\xi\right)\geq 2\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\omega}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}\wedge\xi\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\omega_{1}}\left(\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}\wedge\xi\right)+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\omega_{2}}\left(\|{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}\wedge\xi\right),

where
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\omega}
means that the parameter
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is treated as a random vector with the prior distribution
\omega
.

Step 2. Remove the impact of
\xi
.
Define the following estimators: for
t\in[T]
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}:=\begin{cases}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t},&\text{ if }\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\xi/4},\\
0,&\text{ if }\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}\|_{2}>\sqrt{\xi/4}.\end{cases}

If
\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\xi/16}
, we have the following two cases:

i)

If
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\xi/4}
, then
\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}=\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}
;

ii)

If
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}\|_{2}>\sqrt{\xi/4}
, then

\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\xi/16}=\sqrt{\xi/4}-\sqrt{\xi/16}\leq\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}
.

That is, if
\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\xi/16}
, we always have
\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\leq\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}
.

By Definition
5
, under the prior
\omega_{1}(s_{0},\sqrt{\xi/16})
, we have
\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\xi/16}
with probability 1. By Definition
6
, under the prior
\omega_{2}(s_{0},\sqrt{\xi/32},\tilde{\delta})
, we have
\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}=\sqrt{\xi/32+2\tilde{\delta}^{2}}\leq\sqrt{\xi/16}
with probability 1, where the last inequality is because
\tilde{\delta}^{2}\leq\xi/64
since
T\geq 2\sigma^{2}\xi^{-1}s_{0}\log d
and
L_{0}>1
. As a result, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\omega_{\ell}}\left(\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}\wedge\xi\right)\geq\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\omega_{\ell}}\left(\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}\wedge\xi\right),\text{ for }t\in[T],\;\ell\in\{1,2\}.

In addition, for
\|\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\xi/16}
, we also have
\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2(\xi/4+\xi/16)\leq\xi
, which implies

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\omega_{\ell}}\left(\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}\wedge\xi\right)\geq\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\omega_{\ell}}\left(\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}\right),\text{ for }t\in[T],\;\ell\in\{1,2\}.

Step 3. Lower bound the Bayes risks.
By Lemma
15
and Lemma
17
, for some constant
C>0
, depending only on
\xi
and
L_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\ell=1}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}\sim\omega_{\ell}}\left(\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\geq

\displaystyle C^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}s_{0}}{t+\sigma^{2}s_{0}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{T}s_{0}\log\frac{d}{s_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle C^{-1}\sigma^{2}s_{0}\left(\log\left(\frac{T+\sigma^{2}s_{0}}{2+\sigma^{2}s_{0}}\right)+\log\frac{d}{s_{0}}\right).

The proof is complete.
∎

C.3
Proof of Theorem
5

Let
t\in[T]
and
j\in[d]
. Recall that
\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}
(resp.
\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],j}
) is the submatrix of
\boldsymbol{X}_{[t]}
with columns indexed by
S
(resp.
\{j\}
). Further,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{t}
is the Lasso estimator at time
t
, and denote by
\hat{S}_{t}
the support of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{t}
.

Define the following events:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1,t}=\left\{\frac{L_{0}^{-1}}{2}\leq\lambda_{\min}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}}{t}\right)\leq\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}}{t}\right)\leq\frac{3L_{0}}{2}\right\},

(23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2,t}=\left\{\hat{S}_{t}\subseteq S\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{3,t}=\left\{\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{\infty}\leq\left(\sqrt{L_{0}}+2L_{0}s_{0}\right)\lambda_{t}\right\},

where recall that
L_{0}
and
L_{2}
appears in Assumptions
1 (c)
and
3
.

Lemma 20
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1 (a)
,
1 (c)
, and
3
hold. Further, assume
d\geq 8
and for some constant
C_{0}>0
,
\lambda_{t}
satisfies the following:

\displaystyle 1\geq\lambda_{t}\geq\frac{16\sigma}{1-L_{2}^{-1}}\sqrt{\frac{2\log(d)}{t}},\quad\text{ for }\;t\geq C_{0}s_{0}^{2}\log d,

(24)

Then there exists some constant
C_{T}\geq C_{0}
, that only depends on
L_{0},L_{2}
, such that for all
t\geq C_{T}s_{0}^{2}\log(d)
, we have
\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{3}\mathcal{A}_{i,t}\right)\geq 1/5
.

The proof of Lemma
20
is presented after the proof of Theorem
5
.

Recall the events defined in (
23
). Assume
d\geq 8
. By Lemma
20
, there exists some constant
C_{T}\geq C_{0}
, that only depends on
L_{0},L_{2}
, such that for all
t\geq C_{T}s_{0}^{2}\log(d)
, we have
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{A}_{t}\right)\geq 1/5
, where
\mathcal{A}_{t}:=\bigcap_{i=1}^{3}\mathcal{A}_{i,t}
. Without loss of generality, assume the subset
S
in Assumption
3
is
[s_{0}]
.

For an integer
\ell\geq 1
, let
\mathbf{0}_{\ell}
and
\mathbf{1}_{\ell}
denote the all-zeros and all-ones vector of length
\ell
respectively. Define

\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}=(2\left(\sqrt{L_{0}}+2L_{0}s_{0}\right)\mathbf{1}_{s_{0}}^{\prime},\;\mathbf{0}_{d-s_{0}}^{\prime})^{\prime},

and we focus on the case
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
and
t\geq C_{T}s_{0}^{2}\log(d)
.

By the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{2,t}
and
\mathcal{A}_{3,t}
and since
\lambda_{t}\leq 1
, when
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}
, we have that on the event
\mathcal{A}_{t}
,

(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{t})_{j}>0\;\;\text{ for }j\in S,\quad\text{ so that }\quad\hat{S}_{t}=S,

which implies that

(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{t})_{S}=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{0}}}\left\{\frac{1}{2t}\|\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda_{t}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{1}\right\}.

As a result, on the event
\mathcal{A}_{t}
, we have

\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}}{t}[(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*})_{S}]=\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{[t]}}{t}-\lambda_{t}\boldsymbol{1}_{s_{0}},

where
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{[t]}:=(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{t})^{\prime}
. Thus,
\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}\wedge\xi\geq I_{t}-II_{t}
, where

\displaystyle I_{t}:=\frac{\lambda_{t}^{2}}{2}\left\|\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}}{t}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{1}_{s_{0}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\wedge\xi,\quad II_{t}:=\|(\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S})^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{[t]}\|_{2}^{2}.

On the event
\mathcal{A}_{t}
(in particular,
\mathcal{A}_{1,t}
) and due to (
24
), we have

\displaystyle I_{t}\geq\frac{\lambda_{t}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{1}_{s_{0}}\|_{2}^{2}}{2\left[\lambda_{\max}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}/t\right)\right]^{2}}\wedge\xi\geq\frac{s_{0}}{2(3L_{0}/2)^{2}}\left(\frac{16\sigma}{1-L_{2}^{-1}}\right)^{2}\frac{2\log(d)}{t}\wedge\xi.

Thus, there exists some
C_{T}^{\prime}\geq C_{T}
depending on
L_{0},L_{2},\sigma^{2},\xi
and some
C^{\prime}>0
depending only on
L_{0},L_{2}
, such that for
t\geq C_{T}^{\prime}s_{0}^{2}\log(d)
, on the event
\mathcal{A}_{t}
, we have
I_{t}\geq(C^{\prime})^{-1}\sigma^{2}s_{0}\log(d)/t
.

Further, note that
\mathcal{A}_{1,t}
only depend on
\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}
and thus is dependent from
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{[t]}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}[II_{t};\mathcal{A}_{1,t}]=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}\left[\text{trace}\left((\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S})^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{[t]}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{[t]}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}(\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S})^{-1}\right);\mathcal{A}_{1,t}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}\left[\text{trace}\left((\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S})^{-1}\right);\mathcal{A}_{1,t}\right]\leq\sigma^{2}\frac{s_{0}}{t}2L_{0}.

If
\log(d)\geq 20L_{0}C^{\prime}
, then
\mathbb{E}[II_{t};\mathcal{A}_{1,t}]\leq(10C^{\prime})^{-1}\sigma^{2}s_{0}\log(d)/t
.

To sum up, if
\log(d)\geq 20L_{0}C^{\prime}
, for
t\geq C_{T}^{\prime}s_{0}^{2}\log(d)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}\left(\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\right\|_{2}^{2}\wedge\xi\right)\geq\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}\left[I_{t}-II_{t};\mathcal{A}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(C^{\prime})^{-1}\sigma^{2}\frac{s_{0}\log(d)}{t}\times\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}(\mathcal{A}_{t})-(10C^{\prime})^{-1}\frac{\sigma^{2}s_{0}\log(d)}{t}\geq(10C^{\prime})^{-1}\frac{\sigma^{2}s_{0}\log(d)}{t},

where for the last inequality, we use the fact that
\mathbb{P}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}(\mathcal{A}_{t})\geq 1/5
.

Thus if
T\geq(C_{T}^{\prime}s_{0}^{2}\log(d))^{\kappa}:=t_{0}^{\kappa}
for some
k>1
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}(\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\wedge\xi)\geq\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}}\sum_{t=t_{0}}^{T}(\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\textup{Lasso}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\wedge\xi)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(10C^{\prime})^{-1}\sigma^{2}s_{0}\log d\log\frac{T}{t_{0}}\geq(10C^{\prime})^{-1}\sigma^{2}(1-1/\kappa)s_{0}\log d\log T.

The proof is complete.
∎

Now, we prove Lemma
20
. Recall the events
\mathcal{A}_{i,t}
for
i\in[3]
in (
23
).

For
t\in[T]
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{4,t}=\left\{\max_{j\in{S^{c}}}\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],j}\right\|_{1}\leq\frac{1}{2}(1+L_{2}^{-1})\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{5,t}=\left\{\max_{j\in[d]}\ t^{-1}\|\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],j}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\right\}.

On the event
\mathcal{A}_{1,t}
, we have

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}}{t}\right)^{-1}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}\leq 2L_{0}s_{0}.

Due to (
24
), we have

\delta:=\frac{1-L_{2}^{-1}}{8\sigma}\lambda_{t}-\sqrt{\frac{2\log(d-s_{0})}{t}}\geq\sqrt{\frac{2\log(d)}{t}}.

Since the events
\mathcal{A}_{1,t},\mathcal{A}_{4,t}
and
{\mathcal{A}_{5,t}}
only depend on
\boldsymbol{X}_{[t]}
,
by Corollary 7.22 in
[
45
]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{A}_{2,t}\cap\mathcal{A}_{3,t}\;|\;\mathcal{A}_{1,t}\cap\mathcal{A}_{4,t}\cap{\mathcal{A}_{5,t}}\right)\geq 1-4\exp(-t\delta^{2}/2)\geq 1-4/d.

Since
d\geq 8
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{A}_{1,t}\cap\mathcal{A}_{2,t}\cap\mathcal{A}_{3,t}\right)\geq(1-4/d)\times\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{A}_{1,t}\cap\mathcal{A}_{4,t}\cap{\mathcal{A}_{5,t}}\right)\geq 1/2\times\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{A}_{1,t}\cap\mathcal{A}_{4,t}\cap{\mathcal{A}_{5,t}}\right).

In Steps 1-3, we show that there exists some constant
C_{T}>0
, depending on
L_{0},L_{2}
, such that if
t\geq C_{T}s_{0}^{2}\log(d)
, then

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{A}_{i,t}^{c}\right)\leq 1/5,\quad\text{ for }i=1,4,5.

Then the proof is complete since this would imply
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{A}_{1,t}\cap\mathcal{A}_{4,t}\cap{\mathcal{A}_{5,t}}\right)\geq 2/5
.

Step 1: the event
\mathcal{A}_{1,t}
.
By Lemma
19
, there exists
C>0
depending on
L_{0}
such that for all
t\geq Cs_{0}
, we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}_{1,t}^{c})\leq 1/10
.

Step 2: the event
\mathcal{A}_{5,t}
.
Let
j\in[d]
. Without loss of generality, assume
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j,j}>0
. Due to Assumption
1 (a)
,

\mathbb{P}\left(t^{-1}{\|\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],j}\|_{2}^{2}}>2\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left((t\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j,j})^{-1}\|\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],j}\|_{2}^{2}-1\geq 1\right).

Since the entries of
(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j,j})^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],j}
are i.i.d. standard Gaussians
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, by Example 2.11 in
[
45
]
and the union bound, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{A}_{5,t}^{c}\right)\leq de^{-t/8}.

Thus if
t\geq 8\log(5d)
, then
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{A}_{5,t}^{c}\right)\leq 1/5
.

Step 3: the event
\mathcal{A}_{4,t}
.
For
t\in[T]
and
j\in S^{c}
, we define the following
t
-dimensional vector:

\boldsymbol{Z}_{[t]}^{(j)}=\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],j}-\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S,S}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S,j}.

Since
\boldsymbol{X}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{X}_{t}
are i.i.d. distributed as
\mathcal{N}(0,\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
, we have that

\boldsymbol{Z}_{[t]}^{(j)}=(Z_{1}^{(j)},\ldots,Z_{t}^{(j)})^{\prime}
are independent from
\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}
, and further that
Z_{1}^{(j)},\ldots,Z^{(j)}_{t}
are i.i.d. distributed as
\mathcal{N}(0,b_{j}^{2})
, where

b_{j}^{2}:=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j,j}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{j,S}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S,S})^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S,j}
.

Fix some
j\in S^{c}
. Note that

\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],j}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S,S}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{S,j}+\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}_{[t]}^{(j)}.

Let
\alpha:=2^{-1}(1-L_{2}^{-1})
. Due to Assumption
3
and
\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{s_{0}}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}
for any
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{s_{0}}
, we have
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4,t}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{4,t}
, where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4,t}:=\bigcap_{j\in S^{c}}\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4,t,j},\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4,t,j}:=\left\{\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}_{[t]}^{(j)}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{s_{0}}\alpha^{2}\right\}.

For each
j\in S^{c}
, given
\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}
, the conditional distribution of
(\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S})^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Z}_{[t]}^{(j)}
is the same as
b_{j}(\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S})^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{W}
, where
\boldsymbol{W}
follows the standard
s_{0}
-dimensional normal distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbbm{I}_{s_{0}})
and is independent from any other random variables. Since
\mathcal{A}_{1,t}
only involves
\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4,t,j}^{c}\cap\mathcal{A}_{1,t}\right)=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|(\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S})^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{W}\right\|_{2}^{2}>\frac{\alpha^{2}}{b_{j}^{2}s_{0}}\;|\;\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}\right);\mathcal{A}_{1,t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\boldsymbol{W}\right\|_{2}^{2}>\frac{\alpha^{2}t}{2L_{0}b_{j}^{2}s_{0}}\;|\;\boldsymbol{X}_{[t],S}\right);\mathcal{A}_{1,t}\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{s_{0}}\left\|\boldsymbol{W}\right\|_{2}^{2}>\frac{\alpha^{2}t}{2L_{0}b_{j}^{2}s_{0}^{2}}\right).

Since
b_{j}\leq 1
, by Equation (2.18) and Example 2.11 in
[
45
]
, there exists some
C>0
, depending only on
L_{0},L_{2}
, such that if
t\geq Cs_{0}^{2}\log(d)
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{1}{s_{0}}\left\|\boldsymbol{W}\right\|_{2}^{2}-1>\frac{\alpha^{2}t-2L_{0}b_{j}^{2}s_{0}^{2}}{2L_{0}b_{j}^{2}s_{0}^{2}}\right)\leq 1/(10d).

Then by a union bound and Step 1, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left({\mathcal{A}}_{4,t}^{c}\right)\leq\sum_{j\in S^{c}}\mathbb{P}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{4,t,j}^{c}\cap\mathcal{A}_{1,t}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{A}_{1,t}^{c}\right)\leq 1/5.

∎

D
Proofs for the Bandit Problem - Upper Bounds

In this Appendix, for
t\in[T]
, define

\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{[t]}:=(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{t})^{\prime}.

(25)

Recall that for each time
t\in[T]
and arm
k\in[K]
, we define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}_{[t]}^{(k)}:=\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{1}\mathbb{I}\{A_{1}=k\},\;\cdots,\;\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\mathbb{I}\{A_{t}=k\}\right)^{\prime},

(26)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Y}_{[t]}^{(k)}:=\left(Y_{1}\mathbb{I}\{A_{1}=k\},\;\cdots,\;Y_{t}\mathbb{I}\{A_{t}=k\}\right)^{\prime}.

For each arm
k\in[K]
and
h>0
, let

\mathcal{U}_{h}^{(k)}:=\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{x}>\max_{j\neq k}(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(j)})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{x}+h\}.

(27)

which is the collection of contexts for which the potential reward of
k
-th arm exceeds that of all other arms by more than
h
. For
t\in[T]
and
k\in[K]
, define

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{(k)}:=\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbbm{1}\{\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\in\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(k)}\}\right],\quad\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{t}^{(k)}:=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\boldsymbol{X}_{s}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{s}\mathbbm{1}\{A_{s}=k\},\end{split}

(28)

where
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(k)}
is the collection of contexts for which the potential reward of
k
-th arm exceeds that of all other arms.

Further, recall that we set the regularization and threshold parameters as

\lambda_{t}=6m_{X}\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\log(dT)}{t}},\quad\text{ and }\quad\lambda^{\textup{OPT}}_{t}=28L_{3}\lambda_{t},

(29)

and for some integers
C_{\gamma_{1}}
and
C_{\gamma_{2}}
, set the end times of Stage 1 and 2, respectively, as

\gamma_{1}=C_{\gamma_{1}}\lceil(\sigma^{2}\vee 1)\rceil s_{0}\lceil\log(dT)\rceil,\quad\text{ and }\quad\gamma_{2}=C_{\gamma_{2}}s_{0}^{4}\gamma_{1}.

(30)

Recall that in Stage 1 (i.e., from time
1
to
\gamma_{1}
), arms are selected uniformly at random from
[K]
. By Lemma
25
, Lemma
26
, Lemma
27
, Lemma
30
and Lemma
35
, there exist constants

C^{*}\geq 1,\text{ and }a^{*},h^{*},\ell_{0}^{*},\ell_{1}^{*}>0\;\text{ depending only on }\;K,m_{X},\alpha_{X},L_{3},m_{\theta},L_{4},

(31)

such that the following statements hold for
\gamma_{1}\geq C^{*}s_{0}\log(dT)
and
n\geq C^{*}s_{0}\log(dT)
:

1.

(Lemma
25
) with probability at least
1-2K/T^{4}
, for each arm
k\in[K]
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\sum_{t=1}^{\gamma_{1}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbbm{1}\{A_{t}=k\}\right)\boldsymbol{v}\geq\frac{1}{4KL_{3}}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2},\quad\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{C}(s_{0},3).

(32)

2.

(Lemma
26
) with probability at least
1-2K/T^{4}
, for each arm
k\in[K]
,

\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbbm{1}\{\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\in\mathcal{U}_{h^{*}}^{(k)}\}\right)\boldsymbol{v}\geq\ell_{0}^{*}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2},\quad\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{C}(s_{0},3),

(33)

where we recall the definition of
\mathcal{U}_{h}^{(k)}
in (
27
).

3.

(Lemma
27
)
for any
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}\leq\ell_{1}^{*}
,
with probability at least
1-2/T^{4}
,

\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbbm{1}\{|\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}|\geq h^{*}/2\}\right)\boldsymbol{v}\leq\frac{K-1}{K}a^{*}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2},\quad\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{C}(s_{0},3)

(34)

where we define

a^{*}=\min\{(\ell_{0}^{*}/K),\;1/(4KL_{3})\}.

(35)

4.

(Lemma
30
) for any
t\in[T]
, with probability at least
1-K/T^{2}
, for each arm
k\in[K]

\displaystyle\|(\boldsymbol{X}_{[t]}^{(k)})_{S_{k}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{[t]}/t\|_{2}\leq C^{*}m_{X}\sigma\sqrt{\frac{3s_{0}\log(dT)}{t}}.

(36)

where
(\boldsymbol{X}_{[t]}^{(k)})_{S_{k}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{[t]}=0
if
S_{k}
is empty.

5.

(Lemma
35
) for any
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\boldsymbol{u}\neq\boldsymbol{0}_{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(|\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{1}|\leq\tau)\leq\frac{C^{*}}{\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{2}}\tau,\text{ for all }\tau>0.

(37)

Note that the events in (
33
), (
34
), and (
37
) depend only on the properties of the i.i.d. random vectors
\{\boldsymbol{X}_{t}:t\in[T]\}
, and not on the arm selection mechanism. Moreover, the fourth property above, concerning the event in (
36
), holds regardless of the arm selection mechanism, while the first property pertains specifically to the first stage, in which arms are selected at random. In this Appendix, we fix the constants in (
31
).

D.1
Overall strategy for proving Theorem
11

Theorem
11
follows directly from Lemma
21
and Lemma
23
ahead. Here, we briefly outline the strategy behind the proofs of these two lemmas.

For Lemma
21
concerning Stage 2, we use induction to show that for each
m\in\{1,2,\dots,\lfloor\gamma_{2}/\gamma_{1}\rfloor\}
, with probability at least
1-(6m-2)K/T^{4}
, the following hold:
(i) for each
k\in[K]
, the matrix
\boldsymbol{X}_{[m\gamma_{1}]}^{(k)}
satisfies the restricted eigenvalue (R.E.) condition in Definition
4
with appropriate constants (denoted by the event
\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\textup{RE}}
); and
(ii) for each
k\in[K]
,

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{m\gamma_{1}}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq C\frac{\sigma^{2}s_{0}\log(dT)}{m\gamma_{1}}

for some constant
C>0
(denoted by the event
\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
). Note that R.E.-type conditions are commonly used to derive
\ell_{2}
-estimation error bounds for Lasso estimators
[
45
]
.

Specifically, for the base case
m=1
, the claim holds if
C_{\gamma_{1}}
in (
30
) is chosen sufficiently large. In this case, since arms are selected uniformly at random during Stage 1, the analysis involves i.i.d. observations.

Next, we discuss the inductive step. On the event
\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\textup{RE}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
, we have control over the restricted eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{X}_{[m\gamma_{1}]}^{(k)}
,
k\in[K]
, as well as the estimation error of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{m\gamma_{1}}^{(k)}
,
k\in[K]
. For
t\in(m\gamma_{1},(m+1)\gamma_{1}]
, by Definition
3
, the estimators at time
m\gamma_{1}
are used in arm selections. Thus, the control over estimation errors allows us to show that if the covariate vectors
\{\boldsymbol{X}_{t}:t\in(m\gamma_{1},(m+1)\gamma_{1}]\}
do not fall “too close” to the boundaries, that is,
\cup_{i\neq j\in[K]}\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(i)})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{x}=(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(j)})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{x}\}
, then the selected arm
A_{t}
is equal to the optimal one
A_{t}^{*}:=\argmax_{k\in[K]}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}
. Note that the optimal arms
\{A_{t}^{*}:t\in[T]\}
are i.i.d. random variables. Further, as discussed following Assumption
4
, we use the assumption that
\boldsymbol{X}_{t}
has a log-concave density and bounded eigenvalues to show that the probability that
\{\boldsymbol{X}_{t}:t\in(m\gamma_{1},(m+1)\gamma_{1}]\}
are “close” to the boundaries is small.
These points allow us to show that on the event
\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\textup{RE}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
,
\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\textup{RE}}
holds with high probability. Finally, on
\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\textup{RE}}
, due to the choice of regularization parameter
\lambda_{(m+1)\gamma_{1}}
in (
29
), we can establish upper bound on the
\ell_{2}
estimation error of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{(k)}_{(m+1)\gamma_{1}},k\in[K]
, and thus
\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
holds with high probability. Detailed arguments are provided in Appendix
D.2
.

Finally, we discuss Lemma
23
concerning Stage 3. As in Lemma
21
, we use an inductive argument with similar intuitions as before. The key difference is that our goal is to establish
instance-specific
upper bounds on the
\ell_{2}
estimation errors of the OPT-Lasso estimators, which, in turn, depend on upper bounds for the
\ell_{\infty}
estimation error of the initial Lasso estimators. For the sequential estimation problem in Section
2
, the observations
\{(\boldsymbol{X}_{t},Y_{t}):t\in[T]\}
are i.i.d., and
\boldsymbol{X}_{t}
follows a multivariate normal distribution, for which the recent
\ell_{\infty}
bounds in
[
9
]
apply when
t=\Omega(s_{0}\log(dT))
. However, for the bandit problem, due to the dependency among observations, we resort to the classical
\ell_{\infty}
bounds in Lemma 4.1 of
[
42
]
(see also Lemma
12
), and can only establish the required conditions for
t=\Omega(s_{0}^{5}\log(dT))
. This explains the gap between Stage 1 and Stage 3.
Moreover, this requires us to include additional events in the inductive argument, such as the
\ell_{\infty}
distance between the design matrix for the
k
-th arm and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{(k)}
in (
28
), for
k\in[K]
, as detailed in Appendix
D.4
.

D.2
Proof of Theorem
11
, Part (i): Stage 2

In this subsection, we focus on Stage 2 of the procedure defined in Definition
3
and establish part (i) of Theorem
11
, which follows immediately from Lemma
21
.

Since this subsection focuses on Stage 2, the end time
\gamma_{2}
of Stage 2 can be arbitrary, provided that
\gamma_{2}\geq\gamma_{1}
. Recall the definition of the restricted eigenvalue condition, as well as the set
\mathcal{C}(s,\kappa)
, in Definition
4
.

Recall the fixed constants
C^{*}\geq 1
and
a^{*},h^{*},\ell_{0}^{*},\ell_{1}^{*}>0
in (
31
), which only depend only on
K,m_{X},\alpha_{X},L_{3},m_{\theta},L_{4}
, such that (
32
)-(
37
) hold.

Lemma 21
.

Suppose that Assumptions
4 (a)
,
4 (b)
and
5
hold. We set the regularization parameter
\lambda_{t}
as in (
29
), and the end time
\gamma_{1}
of Stage 1 as in (
30
).
If the integer
C_{\gamma_{1}}
in (
30
) satisfies the following:

\displaystyle C_{\gamma_{1}}\geq\max\left\{C^{*},\;\left(\frac{18m_{X}}{\ell_{1}^{*}a^{*}}\right)^{2}\right\},

(38)

then for each
m\in\{1,2,\cdots,\lfloor\gamma_{2}/\gamma_{1}\rfloor\}
, with probability at least
1-(6m-2)K/T^{4}
, we have

i)

for each
k\in[K]
,
\boldsymbol{X}_{[m\gamma_{1}]}^{(k)}
satisfies
\textup{RE}(s_{0},3,a^{*})
;

ii)

for each
k\in[K]
,

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{m\gamma_{1}}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{(18m_{X})^{2}}{(a^{*})^{2}}\frac{\sigma^{2}s_{0}\log(dT)}{m\gamma_{1}}.

For integer
m=1,\cdots,\lfloor\gamma_{2}/\gamma_{1}\rfloor
, we define the following events: with
t=m\gamma_{1}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\textup{RE}}:=\bigcap_{k=1}^{K}\left\{\boldsymbol{X}_{[t]}^{(k)}\textup{ satisfies }\textup{RE}(s_{0},3,a^{*})\right\},\\
&\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}=\bigcap_{k=1}^{K}\left\{\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{9}{(a^{*})^{2}}s_{0}\lambda_{t}^{2}\right\},\\
&\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}=\bigcap_{k\in[K]}\left\{\|{(\boldsymbol{X}_{[t]}^{(k)}})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{[t]}/t\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\lambda_{t}}{2}\right\}.\end{split}

(39)

The proof proceeds by induction to show that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\textup{RE}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})\geq 1-(6m-2)K/T^{4}
, for
m=1,\ldots,\lfloor\gamma_{2}/\gamma_{1}\rfloor
.

Base case: m = 1.
Due to (
32
) and the definition of
a^{*}
in (
35
), we have
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\textup{RE}}\right)\geq 1-2K/T^{4}
. Further, due to Lemma
29
and the definition of
\lambda_{m\gamma_{1}}
in (
29
),
we have
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\right)\geq 1-2K/T^{4}
. Finally, on the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\textup{RE}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
,
due to the second inequality in Lemma
12
, we have that the event
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
holds. Thus, by the union bound,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\textup{RE}})

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\textup{RE}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}^{\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}})\geq 1-\frac{4K}{T^{4}}.

The base case is established.

Inductive step.
Now, we assume that for
1\leq m<\lfloor\gamma_{2}/\gamma_{1}\rfloor
,

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\textup{RE}})\geq 1-\frac{(6m-2)K}{T^{4}}
. Our goal is to show that

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\textup{RE}})\geq 1-\frac{(6m+4)K}{T^{4}}
.

On the event
\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\textup{RE}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
,
due to the second inequality in Lemma
12
, we have that the event
\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
holds. Thus, by the union bound,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\textup{RE}})\geq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\textup{RE}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}})\geq 1-\mathbb{P}((\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\textup{RE}})^{c})-\mathbb{P}((\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}})^{c}).

By Lemma
29
and the definition of
\lambda_{(m+1)\gamma_{1}}
in (
29
),
we have
\mathbb{P}\left((\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}})^{c}\right)\leq 2K/T^{4}
. Thus, it suffices to show that

\mathbb{P}((\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\textup{RE}})^{c})\leq(6m+2)K/T^{4}
, on which we now focus.

For
m\gamma_{1}<t\leq(m+1)\gamma_{1}
and
k\in[K]
, define the event

\mathcal{B}_{t}^{(k)}=\left(\bigcap_{i\in[K]}\left\{|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{m\gamma_{1}}^{(i)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(i)})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}|\leq{h^{*}}/{2}\right\}\right)\bigcap\left\{\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\in\mathcal{U}_{h^{*}}^{(k)}\right\}.

(40)

Due to the definition of
\mathcal{U}_{h}^{(k)}
in (
27
), for any
j\neq k\in[K]
, on the event
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{(k)}
,

\displaystyle(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{m\gamma_{1}}^{(k)}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{m\gamma_{1}}^{(j)})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}

\displaystyle\geq(\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(j)})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}-|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{m\gamma_{1}}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}|-|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{m\gamma_{1}}^{(j)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(j)})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}|

\displaystyle>h^{*}-h^{*}/2-h^{*}/2=0.

which implies that
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{(k)}\subseteq\{A_{t}=k\}
, and thus for each
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{C}(s_{0},3)
,

\displaystyle\frac{\|\boldsymbol{X}_{[(m+1)\gamma_{1}]}^{(k)}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}}{(m+1)\gamma_{1}}\geq\frac{m\gamma_{1}}{(m+1)\gamma_{1}}\frac{\|\boldsymbol{X}_{[m\gamma_{1}]}^{(k)}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}}{m\gamma_{1}}+\frac{\gamma_{1}}{(m+1)\gamma_{1}}\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\sum_{t=m\gamma_{1}+1}^{(m+1)\gamma_{1}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbbm{1}{\{\mathcal{B}_{t}^{(k)}\}}\right)\boldsymbol{v}.

Due to the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\textup{RE}}
in (
39
) and
\mathcal{B}_{t}^{(k)}
in (
40
), on the event
\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\textup{RE}}
, we have that for each
k\in[K]
and
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{C}(s_{0},3)
,

\displaystyle\frac{\|\boldsymbol{X}_{[(m+1)\gamma_{1}]}^{(k)}\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}}{(m+1)\gamma_{1}}\geq\frac{m}{m+1}a^{*}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{m+1}\left(I_{m+1}^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{v})-\sum_{i\in[K]}II_{m+1}^{(i)}(\boldsymbol{v})\right),

(41)

where we define

\displaystyle I_{m+1}^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{v}):=\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\sum_{t=m\gamma_{1}+1}^{(m+1)\gamma_{1}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbbm{1}\{\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\in\mathcal{U}_{h^{*}}^{(k)}\}\right)\boldsymbol{v},

\displaystyle II_{m+1}^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{v}):=\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}\left(\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\sum_{t=m\gamma_{1}+1}^{(m+1)\gamma_{1}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\prime}\mathbbm{1}\{|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{m\gamma_{1}}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}|\geq h^{*}/2\}\right)\boldsymbol{v}.

Further, we define the following two events

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{m+1}:=\bigcap_{k\in[K]}\left\{I_{m+1}^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{v})\geq\ell_{0}^{*}\|\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}\;\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{C}(s_{0},3)\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{m+1}=\bigcap_{k=1}^{K}\left\{II_{m+1}^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{v})\leq\frac{K-1}{K}a^{*}\|v\|_{2}^{2}\;\text{ for all }\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathcal{C}(s_{0},3)\right\}.

By the definition of
a^{*}
in (
35
),
\ell_{0}^{*}\geq a^{*}K
. Thus, due to (
41
) and the definition
\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\textup{RE}}
in (
39
),

\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\textup{RE}}\bigcap\mathcal{A}_{m+1}\bigcap\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{m+1}\;\subseteq\;\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\textup{RE}}

Finally, due to (
33
), we have that
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{A}_{m+1}\right)\geq 1-2K/T^{4}
. Further, on the event
\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}
defined in (
39
) and
\lambda_{m\gamma_{1}}
in (
29
), due to (
38
), for each
k\in[K]
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{m\gamma_{1}}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{9}{(a^{*})^{2}}s_{0}(6m_{X}\sigma)^{2}\frac{\log(dT)}{m\gamma_{1}}\leq\frac{9}{(a^{*})^{2}}\frac{(6m_{X})^{2}}{C_{\gamma_{1}}}\leq(\ell_{1}^{*})^{2}.

Thus, since
\{\boldsymbol{X}_{t}:m\gamma_{1}<t\leq(m+1)\gamma_{1}\}
are independent from
\mathcal{F}_{m\gamma_{1}}
and
\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\in\mathcal{F}_{m\gamma_{1}}
, due to (
34
) with
\boldsymbol{u}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{m\gamma_{1}}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}
, we have that almost surely

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{m+1}|\mathcal{F}_{m\gamma_{1}}\right)\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\}\geq(1-2K/T^{4})\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\}.

Combining the above results, by the union bound, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left((\mathcal{E}_{m+1}^{\textup{RE}})^{c}\right)\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left((\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\textup{RE}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}})^{c}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left((\mathcal{A}_{m+1})^{c}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left((\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{m+1})^{c}\;|\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\textup{RE}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(6m-2)K/T^{4}+2K/T^{4}+2K/T^{4}=(6m+2)K/T^{4},

where we use the inductive hypothesis on
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\textup{RE}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\boldsymbol{\theta}}\right)
in the second step. Then the inductive step is finished, and the proof is complete.
∎

D.3
Additional results for Stage 2

The following lemma concerns the property of

\boldsymbol{X}_{[t]}^{(k)}
in (
26
), the Lasso estimator
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{t}^{(k)}
in Subsection
3.1
, and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(k)}_{t}
in (
28
) at the end of Stage 2, that is, for
t=\gamma_{2}
. Recall the definition of
\mathcal{U}_{h}^{(k)}
in (
27
) and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{(k)}
in (
28
).

Recall the fixed constants
C^{*}\geq 1
and
a^{*},h^{*},\ell_{0}^{*},\ell_{1}^{*}>0
in (
31
), which only depend only on
K,m_{X},\alpha_{X},L_{3},m_{\theta},L_{4}
, such that (
32
)-(
37
) hold.

Lemma 22
.

Suppose that Assumptions
4
and
5
hold. We set the regularization parameter
\lambda_{t}
as in (
29
), and the end time
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
of Stage 1 and 2, respectively, as in (
30
).
If the integers
C_{\gamma_{1}}
and
C_{\gamma_{2}}
in (
30
) satisfy the following:

\displaystyle C_{\gamma_{1}}\geq\max\left\{C^{*},\;\left(\frac{18m_{X}}{\ell_{1}^{*}a^{*}}\right)^{2},2\right\},\;\;C_{\gamma_{2}}\geq\max\left\{4,\frac{32m_{X}^{4}}{(a^{*})^{2}},\left(\frac{192KL_{4}C^{*}m_{X}^{4}}{(a^{*})^{2}}\right)^{4}\right\}.

(42)

then, with probability at least
1-17K/T^{2}
, the event
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\textup{RE}}\cap\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\cap\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\textup{Lasso}}
occurs, where

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\textup{RE}}:=\bigcap_{k=1}^{K}\left\{\boldsymbol{X}_{[\gamma_{2}]}^{(k)}\textup{ satisfies }\textup{RE}(s_{0},3,a^{*})\right\},\\
&\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}:=\bigcap_{k=1}^{K}\left\{|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\gamma_{2}}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{(k)}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{\textup{max}}\leq\frac{3a^{*}}{s_{0}}\right\},\\
&\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\textup{Lasso}}=\bigcap_{k=1}^{K}\left\{\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\gamma_{2}}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}\leq 28L_{3}\lambda_{\gamma_{2}}\right\}.\end{split}

Recall the events
\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\textup{RE}}
and
\mathcal{E}_{m}^{\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
in (
39
) for
m=1,\ldots,\gamma_{2}/\gamma_{1}
, where
\gamma_{2}
is now a multiple of
\gamma_{1}
. Define the following times and ratios

\displaystyle\widetilde{\gamma}_{1}:=\lceil\sqrt{C_{\gamma_{2}}}\rceil s_{0}^{3}\gamma_{1},\quad r:=\frac{\widetilde{\gamma}_{1}}{\gamma_{1}},\quad R:=\frac{\gamma_{2}}{\gamma_{1}}.

(43)

By Lemma
21
and
Lemma
29

for each
m=1,2,\cdots,R
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{k=1}^{K}\left\{\boldsymbol{X}_{[m\gamma_{1}]}^{(k)}\textup{ satisfies }\textup{RE}(s_{0},3,a^{*})\right\}\right)\geq 1-(6m-2)K/T^{4},\\
&\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{k=1}^{K}\left\{\|(\boldsymbol{X}_{[m\gamma_{1}]}^{(k)})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{[m\gamma_{1}]}^{(k)}/(m\gamma_{1})\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda_{m\gamma_{1}}/2\right\}\right)\geq 1-2K/T^{4}.\end{split}

(44)

Note that
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\textup{RE}}=\mathcal{E}_{R}^{\textup{RE}}
, and thus
\mathbb{P}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\textup{RE}}\right)\geq 1-6K/T^{2}.
Further, applying Lemma
12

with
\boldsymbol{A}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{(k)}
, and due to Assumption
4 (c)
, on the event
\mathcal{E}_{R}^{\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\cap\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\textup{RE}}\cap\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
, for each
k\in[K]
,

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\gamma_{2}}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{(k)}\|_{\infty}\leq L_{3}\left(4+\frac{8s_{0}}{a^{*}}\times\frac{3a^{*}}{s_{0}}\right)\lambda_{\gamma_{2}}=28L_{3}\lambda_{\gamma_{2}}.

That is,
\mathcal{E}_{R}^{\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\cap\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\textup{RE}}\cap\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\;\subseteq\;\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\textup{Lasso}}
.
Thus, by the union bound, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\textup{RE}}\cap\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\cap\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\textup{Lasso}}\right)\geq 1-\mathbb{P}((\mathcal{E}_{R}^{\boldsymbol{X}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}})^{c})-\mathbb{P}((\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\textup{RE}})^{c})-\mathbb{P}((\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})^{c})\geq 1-\frac{8K}{T^{2}}-\mathbb{P}((\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})^{c}).

As a result, it suffices to show that
\mathbb{P}((\tilde{\mathcal{E}}_{1}^{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})^{c})\leq 9K/T^{2}
, on which we now focus.

Recall the definition of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\gamma_{2}}^{(k)}
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{(k)}
in (
28
), and
\mathcal{U}_{h}^{(k)}
in (
27
). By the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\gamma_{2}}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{(k)}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\textup{max}}

\displaystyle\leq I^{(k)}+II^{(k)}+III^{(k)},\;\;\text{ for }k\in[K],

(45)

where we define
